Parabolic PDEs with Dynamic Data under a Bounded Slope Condition
Authors: Verena Bögelein, Frank Duzaar, Giulia Treu
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We establish the existence of Lipschitz-continuous solutions to the Cauchy–Dirichlet problem for a class of evolutionary partial differential equations of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _tu-{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_x \nabla _\xi f(\nabla u)=0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-divx∇ξf(∇u)=0in a space-time cylinder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _T=\Omega \times (0,T)$$\end{document}ΩT=Ω×(0,T), subject to time-dependent boundary data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:\partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}g:∂PΩT→R prescribed on the parabolic boundary. The main novelty in our analysis is a time-dependent version of the classical bounded slope condition, imposed on the boundary data g along the lateral boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega \times (0,T)$$\end{document}∂Ω×(0,T). More precisely, we require that, for each fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T), the graph of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\cdot ,t)$$\end{document}g(·,t) over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega $$\end{document}∂Ω admits supporting hyperplanes with slopes that may vary in time but remain uniformly bounded. The key to handling time-dependent data lies in constructing more flexible upper and lower barriers.

Introduction

Throughout this paper, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Ω⊂Rn, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, denote a bounded, open, and convex set, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f :\mathbb {R}^n \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Rn→R be a convex integrand. A classical theorem of Haar [24] ensures that, for prescribed boundary values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_o :\partial \Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}uo:∂Ω→R satisfying the bounded slope condition (BSC), there exists a Lipschitz continuous minimizer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u :\Omega \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u:Ω→R of the variational functional1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}(v) := \int _{\Omega } f(Dv) \,\textrm{d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(v):=∫Ωf(Dv)dx,subject to the boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u = u_o$$\end{document}u=uo on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega $$\end{document}∂Ω; see also [25, 26, 36, 40]. The construction has become standard and appears in modern textbooks on the calculus of variations; see, e.g., [22, Chapter 1]. More recent developments concerning the existence of Lipschitz minimizers under the BSC may be found in [10, 12, 13, 15, 31–34]. The standard argument hinges on a comparison principle in which the affine functions furnished by the BSC serve as barriers, together with the translation invariance of minimizers and a priori gradient bounds. The successful application of this strategy depends sensitively on the structure of the integrand, and in particular breaks down when lower-order terms are present. In such cases, affine functions no longer provide suitable barriers, and the method must be adapted by employing a more flexible class of barrier functions; see [19–21].

Surprisingly, a time-dependent counterpart to this semi-classical theory has remained an open problem for some time. Instead, sophisticated parabolic methods – such as Galerkin approximations, monotone operators, and nonlinear semigroup theory – have led to various existence results. However, the construction of Lipschitz continuous solutions to evolutionary equations associated with general convex integrands f has remained elusive without additional assumptions on the growth of the integrand. The paper [6] marks the first Haar-Rado-type result for the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cc} \partial _tu-{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_x \nabla _\xi f(\nabla u)=0, & \quad \text{ in } \Omega _T=\Omega \times (0,T),\\ u=u_o, & \quad \text{ on } \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-divx∇ξf(∇u)=0,inΩT=Ω×(0,T),u=uo,on∂PΩT,which guarantees the existence of a unique classical solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^0([0,T];L^2(\Omega )) \cap L^\infty (\Omega _T)$$\end{document}u∈C0([0,T];L2(Ω))∩L∞(ΩT) with Lipschitz continuous spatial gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla u\in L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)$$\end{document}∇u∈L∞(ΩT,Rn). The only conditions required are the convexity of the integrand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R}^n\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Rn→R, and the BSC imposed on the initial and lateral boundary datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_o\in W^{1,\infty }(\Omega )$$\end{document}uo∈W1,∞(Ω). A related result for linear growth functionals can be found in [23]; see also [44]. The theory has subsequently been further generalized to data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_o$$\end{document}uo satisfying a one-sided BSC, as shown in [11, 37, 41]. A common feature of all these parabolic papers is the use of the BSC to construct affine barriers.

At this juncture, the natural question arises as to whether a semi-classical theory can be developed for time-dependent boundary values. Specifically, one might ask whether it is possible to construct Lipschitz solutions to the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3) with a boundary datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:\partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}g:∂PΩT→R depending on time. As we will discuss later, the proof strategy of [6] cannot, in principle, be applied to time-dependent boundary conditions. The barrier construction employed in [6] necessitates that the boundary values g be independent of time.

Lipschitz Solutions

In the parabolic setting, the formulation of Lipschitz continuous variational solutions involves certain function spaces that can be interpreted as the parabolic analogue of Lipschitz continuous functions from the stationary setting. Specifically, we use the identification between the space of Lipschitz continuous functions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} )$$\end{document}Lipx(Ω)=C0,1(Ω), and the Sobolev space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{1,\infty }(\Omega )$$\end{document}W1,∞(Ω) to define the function space consisting of those bounded functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} For the definition of the notion of variational solution to the parabolic Cauchy-Dirichlet problem1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cc} \partial _tu-{{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_x \nabla _\xi f(\nabla u)=0, & \quad \text{ in } \Omega _T=\Omega \times (0,T),\\ u=g, & \quad \text{ on } \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-divx∇ξf(∇u)=0,inΩT=Ω×(0,T),u=g,on∂PΩTwe follow the approach of Lichnewsky and Temam [30], originally introduced in the context of the time-dependent parametric minimal surface equation. This idea leads naturally to the notion of variational solutions used in what follows.

Definition 1.1

(Variational Solution) Assume that the Cauchy-Dirichlet datum g fulfills hypothesis (1.2). A map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in \textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}u∈Lipgx(ΩT) is called variational solution on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{T}$$\end{document}ΩT to the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3) if and only if the variational inequality1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iint _{\Omega _\tau }f(\nabla u)\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t&\le \iint _{\Omega _\tau }\big [\partial _{t}v(v-u) + f(\nabla v)\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\nonumber \\&\quad + \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert v(0)-g_{o}\Vert _{L^{2}(\Omega )}^{2} - \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert (v-u)(\tau )\Vert _{L^{2}(\Omega )}^{2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∬Ωτf(∇u)dxdt≤∬Ωτ[∂tv(v-u)+f(∇v)]dxdt+12‖v(0)-go‖L2(Ω)2-12‖(v-u)(τ)‖L2(Ω)2holds true, for a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in [0,T]$$\end{document}τ∈[0,T] and for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \textrm{Lip}^x_{g}(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}v∈Lipgx(ΩT) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{t}v\in L^{2}(\Omega _{T})$$\end{document}∂tv∈L2(ΩT) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(0)\in L^2(\Omega )$$\end{document}v(0)∈L2(Ω).

Note that all terms in (1.4) are well-defined, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}u∈Lipgx(ΩT) implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^\infty (0,T; L^2(\Omega ))$$\end{document}u∈L∞(0,T;L2(Ω)). By assumption (1.2), the function g is admissible as a test function in the variational inequality (1.4). This permits testing with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = g$$\end{document}v=g, which yields certain energy estimates. As a consequence, one can conclude that variational solutions satisfy the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\cdot , 0)=g_o$$\end{document}u(·,0)=go in the usual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega )$$\end{document}L2(Ω) sense, as demonstrated in Lemma 3.1. The concept of a variational solution enables the use of techniques from the calculus of variations. Under certain conditions, it can be demonstrated that a variational solution also satisfies the properties of a weak solution; see Theorem 1.4 below.

As in the classical theory of variational problems, we begin by constructing variational solutions subject to a gradient constraint. In this regard, we introduce a notion of variational solution that incorporates such a constraint. Assuming that for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L> 0$$\end{document}L>0, we have1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L>\max \big \{ \Vert \nabla g_o\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega ,\mathbb {R}^n)}, \Vert \nabla g\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)}\big \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L>max{‖∇go‖L∞(Ω,Rn),‖∇g‖L∞(ΩT,Rn)},the subclass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T, L)$$\end{document}Lipgx(ΩT,L) is non-empty, and the following definition of variational solutions with gradient constraints is well-posed:

Definition 1.2

(Variational solutions, gradient constraint) Let g be as in (1.2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L>0$$\end{document}L>0 as in (1.5). A map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in \textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T,L)$$\end{document}u∈Lipgx(ΩT,L) is called variational solution of the gradient constrained Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T,L)$$\end{document}Lipgx(ΩT,L) if and only if the variational inequality1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iint _{\Omega _\tau }f(\nabla u)\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t&\le \iint _{\Omega _\tau }\big [\partial _{t}v(v-u) + f(\nabla v)\big ]\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\nonumber \\&\quad + \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert v(0)-g_{o}\Vert _{L^{2}(\Omega )}^{2} - The Main Results

Our main result in this paper is the existence of Lipschitz continuous solutions (with respect to the spatial variable) to the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3) with time-dependent boundary values, under the assumption of the bounded slope condition. Before stating the main result, we first outline the assumptions on the data. Theorem 1.3

(Existence of Lipschitz solutions) Suppose that assumptions (A1)–(A3) are satisfied. Then there exists a unique variational solution to the Cauchy–Dirichlet problem (1.3) in the sense of Definition 1.1 which satisfies the gradient bound\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \nabla u\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)} \le C, $$\end{document}‖∇u‖L∞(ΩT,Rn)≤C,where C depends on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n, \varepsilon , R, {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(\Omega ), f, \nabla f, \Vert D^2f\Vert _{L^\infty (\mathbb {R}^n\setminus B_1)}, Q, [g]_{0,1;\Omega _T}$$\end{document}n,ε,R,diam(Ω),f,∇f,‖D2f‖L∞(Rn\B1),Q,[g]0,1;ΩT, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathsf Q$$\end{document}Q.

We emphasize that the variational solution is unique even when the integrand f is convex but not strictly convex; see [9, Lemma 3.3]. This includes the case of integrands with flat regions. Moreover, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^1$$\end{document}f∈C1, then the variational solution constructed above enjoys additional regularity.

Theorem 1.4

(Regularity of solutions) Suppose that assumptions (A1)–(A3) are satisfied, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in C^1$$\end{document}f∈C1. Novelty and Key Technical Tools

The proof proceeds in two steps. The first establishes the existence of variational solutions for time-dependent boundary data, under a uniform Lipschitz bound on the admissible class. This reduces to a variational inequality with a gradient constraint. The construction follows De Giorgi’s minimizing movement scheme, cf. [2], which is particularly suited to incorporating time-dependent Dirichlet data on the lateral boundary into the class of admissible competitors, cf. [5, 9, 38]. An alternative derivation, based on the method of weighted energy dissipation, cf. [1, 3, 4, 35], draws on De Giorgi’s variational framework for nonlinear evolution equations, including applications to certain nonlinear wave equations; see [17, 28, 39, 42]. This may be viewed as the first step in the spirit of Haar’s [24] approach to constructing Lipschitz minimizers of the parametric area functional–a perspective that anticipated modern developments in geometric analysis and regularity theory for minimal surfaces.

The solutions obtained are variational solutions in the sense of Lichnewsky and Temam [30]; that is, they solve a variational inequality subject to a gradient constraint of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nabla u|\le L$$\end{document}|∇u|≤L, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L>0$$\end{document}L>0 is a fixed constant. As a result, the class of admissible variations is restricted to those that preserve this structural bound. In particular, the presence of the constraint limits perturbations to directions that remain within the admissible set. It is therefore necessary to establish that the variational solution lies strictly inside the admissible class, in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \nabla u\Vert _{\infty }<L$$\end{document}‖∇u‖∞<L; only then can arbitrary variations be performed, and the solution identified as the sole admissible one satisfying the variational inequality in the unconstrained class.

In the classical setting, this difficulty was addressed by Hilbert [27] through the introduction of the bounded slope condition, later refined by Haar [24] in the construction of Lipschitz minimizers of the parametric area functional. By restricting attention to graphs with uniformly bounded gradient, compactness is restored and direct methods become applicable, obviating the need for unconstrained variations. The bounded slope condition furnishes barriers required for comparison and maximum principles, and renders the explicit gradient constraint superfluous by enforcing uniform control through the prescribed bound.

The use of affine barriers derived from the bounded slope condition fundamentally relies on temporal constancy of the Dirichlet boundary data. This constraint becomes evident upon inspecting the argument in [6, Theorem 1.2], where for a fixed point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o\in \partial \Omega $$\end{document}xo∈∂Ω the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x_o,t)+w_{x_o}^-(t)\cdot (x-x_o)$$\end{document}g(xo,t)+wxo-(t)·(x-xo) is employed as a lower barrier and must act as a sub-solution to (1.3)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}1. Differentiation in time yields the constraint\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t\big (g(x_o,t)+w_{x_o}^-(t)\cdot (x - x_o)\big ) = \partial _t g(x_o,t)+ \partial _t w_{x_o}^-(t)\cdot (x-x_o) \le 0 $$\end{document}∂t(g(xo,t)+wxo-(t)·(x-xo))=∂tg(xo,t)+∂twxo-(t)·(x-xo)≤0for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \Omega _T$$\end{document}(x,t)∈ΩT. Taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow x_o$$\end{document}x→xo within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t g(x_o,t)\le 0$$\end{document}∂tg(xo,t)≤0 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,T)$$\end{document}t∈(0,T). A symmetric argument involving an upper barrier of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x_o,t)+w_{x_o}^+(t)\cdot (x-x_o)$$\end{document}g(xo,t)+wxo+(t)·(x-xo) leads to the inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t g(x_o,t)\ge 0$$\end{document}∂tg(xo,t)≥0. One is thus forced to conclude that g is stationary on the lateral boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega \times (0,T)$$\end{document}∂Ω×(0,T). In the presence of genuinely time-dependent boundary values, such affine constructions therefore fail to apply. While the bounded slope condition retains its structural role, the analysis in the presence of time-dependent boundary data necessitates more flexible constructions: the use of affine sub- or super-solutions, though formally admissible, inherently restricts the boundary values to be time-independent and is therefore incompatible with the temporal variability intrinsic to the problem.

Instead of employing affine barriers, we implement a construction based on the convex conjugate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*$$\end{document}f∗, following an observation due to Cellina [14]. The central idea exploits the fact that, when the integrand f is of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2, a function of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v(x,t) = \frac{n}{\alpha }f^*\Big (\frac{\alpha }{n}\big (x-y(t)\big )\Big )-c(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v(x,t)=nαf∗(αn(x-y(t)))-c(t),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}\setminus \{0\}$$\end{document}α∈R\{0}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y:[0,T]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}y:[0,T]→Rn, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c:[0,T]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c:[0,T]→R satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_x\nabla _\xi f\big (\nabla v(x,t)\big )=\alpha . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}divx∇ξf(∇v(x,t))=α.In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0, then a choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, y, and c such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α dominates the time derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tv$$\end{document}∂tv at every point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \Omega _T$$\end{document}(x,t)∈ΩT would ensure that v is a sub-solution to the parabolic equation (1.3), and thus a potential candidate for a barrier from below. However, being a subsolution is only one of the requirements for a function to qualify as a barrier from below. In addition, it must be compatible with the boundary data in the sense that, for some fixed boundary point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o\in \partial \Omega $$\end{document}xo∈∂Ω, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ v(x_o,t)=g(x_o,t)$$\end{document}v(xo,t)=g(xo,t) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\le g$$\end{document}v≤g on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T$$\end{document}∂PΩT. At this stage, the principal difficulty lies in ensuring that the real parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, together with the functions y and c, can indeed be chosen so that v constitutes a lower barrier. This is realized by considering, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T), the set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\Omega }_t := \bigg \{x\in \mathbb {R}^n: \underbrace{\frac{n}{\alpha }f^*\Big (\frac{\alpha }{n}\big (x-y(t)\big )\Big )-c(t)}_{=\, v(x,t)}\le g(x_o,t)+\widetilde{w}_{x_o}^{-}(t)\cdot (x-x_o)\bigg \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω~t:={x∈Rn:nαf∗(αn(x-y(t)))-c(t)⏟=v(x,t)≤g(xo,t)+w~xo-(t)·(x-xo)},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{w}_{x_o}^{-}$$\end{document}w~xo- is a suitable modification of the affine function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{x_o}^{-}$$\end{document}wxo-, chosen to satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x_o,t)+\widetilde{w}_{x_o}^{-}(t)\cdot (x-x_o)\le g(x,t)$$\end{document}g(xo,t)+w~xo-(t)·(x-xo)≤g(x,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \Omega _T$$\end{document}(x,t)∈ΩT. We then carefully select y(t) and c(t) such that, on the one hand,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_o\in \partial \widetilde{\Omega }_t \quad \text{ for } \text{ all } t\in [0,T), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}xo∈∂Ω~tforallt∈[0,T),and, on the other hand,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega \subset \widetilde{\Omega }_t \quad \text{ for } \text{ all } t\in [0,T). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω⊂Ω~tforallt∈[0,T).By the definition of the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Omega }_t$$\end{document}Ω~t, this implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x_o,t)=g(x_o,t)$$\end{document}v(xo,t)=g(xo,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\le g$$\end{document}v≤g in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _T$$\end{document}ΩT. Next, we establish an upper bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\partial _t v|$$\end{document}|∂tv| in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _T$$\end{document}ΩT that is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. This enables us to choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α sufficiently large to guarantee that v is a subsolution. Finally, we compute an upper bound on the spatial gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla v$$\end{document}∇v, thereby demonstrating that the constructed lower barrier is Lipschitz continuous. Realizing this approach would already be delicate for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-integrands. The weaker assumption (A2) on f requires even greater care to balance the parameters involved and to avoid circular dependencies.

Explicit Construction of a Lower Barrier in a Specific Example

To illustrate the construction of the lower barriers summarized in the preceding subsection and described in detail in § 6, we provide a simple example in which these barriers are explicitly constructed. Thereby, we use the notation introduced in § 1.3 and § 6. Let us consider the Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =B_1(0)\subset \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}Ω=B1(0)⊂R2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(\xi )=\frac{1}{2} |\xi |^2$$\end{document}f(ξ)=12|ξ|2 with convex conjugate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*(\eta )=\frac{1}{2} |\eta |^2$$\end{document}f∗(η)=12|η|2, and boundary data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x,t)=x_1\cos t+x_2\sin t =(\cos t,\sin t)\cdot (x_1,x_2)$$\end{document}g(x,t)=x1cost+x2sint=(cost,sint)·(x1,x2). We observe that, in this case, the boundary values are affine in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(x_1,x_2)$$\end{document}x=(x1,x2) for each fixed t, and they satisfy the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t-\textrm{BSC}_Q$$\end{document}t-BSCQ condition with constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=1$$\end{document}Q=1; see Definition 2.3 below. In particular, we note that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t-\textrm{BSC}_Q$$\end{document}t-BSCQ is satisfied for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o\in \partial B_1(0)$$\end{document}xo∈∂B1(0) with the choice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{x_o}^-(t)=(\cos t,\sin t)$$\end{document}wxo-(t)=(cost,sint). In the setting of this example, the function defined in (6.1) takes the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v(x,t)=\tfrac{\alpha }{4}|x-y(t)|^2-c(t), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v(x,t)=α4|x-y(t)|2-c(t),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0 is a parameter, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y(t)\in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}y(t)∈R2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c(t)\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c(t)∈R are functions of time. We fix the point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o=(-1,0)\in \partial B_1(0)$$\end{document}xo=(-1,0)∈∂B1(0) and aim to show that one can choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, y, and c such that the following conditions are satisfied: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x_o,t)=g(x_o,t)$$\end{document}v(xo,t)=g(xo,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T);\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x,t)\le g(x,t)$$\end{document}v(x,t)≤g(x,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\big (B_1(0)\times [0,T)\big )$$\end{document}(x,t)∈∂P(B1(0)×[0,T));v is a sub-solution of 1.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t v-\Delta v =0 \qquad \text { in } B_1(0)\times [0,T). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv-Δv=0inB1(0)×[0,T).For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0 we now choose y(t) and c(t) such that (C1) and (C2) are satisfied. To this end, we define, cf. § 6,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\Omega }_t&= \big \{x\in \mathbb {R}^2: \tfrac{\alpha }{4}|x-y(t)|^2-c(t)\le g(x_o,t)+w_{x_o}^-(t)\cdot (x-x_o)\big \} \\&= \big \{x\in \mathbb {R}^2: \tfrac{\alpha }{4}|x-y(t)|^2-c(t)\le x_1 \cos t+x_2\sin t\big \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω~t={x∈R2:α4|x-y(t)|2-c(t)≤g(xo,t)+wxo-(t)·(x-xo)}={x∈R2:α4|x-y(t)|2-c(t)≤x1cost+x2sint}.Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x_o,t)+w_{x_o}^-(t)\cdot (x-x_o)= g(x,t)$$\end{document}g(xo,t)+wxo-(t)·(x-xo)=g(x,t) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in B_1(0)\times [0,T)$$\end{document}(x,t)∈B1(0)×[0,T) and hence we can take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{w}_{x_o}^-=w_{x_o}^-$$\end{document}w~xo-=wxo- in Lemma 2.5. Next, we choose\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ y(t)=(y_1(t),y_2(t))=\tfrac{2}{\alpha }\left( 1-\cos t,-\sin t\right) $$\end{document}y(t)=(y1(t),y2(t))=2α1-cost,-sintand\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ c(t)= \tfrac{\alpha }{4}+1+\tfrac{2}{\alpha }\left( 1-\cos t\right) , $$\end{document}c(t)=α4+1+2α1-cost,and demonstrate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o=(-1,0)\in \partial \widetilde{\Omega }_t$$\end{document}xo=(-1,0)∈∂Ω~t and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B(0,1)\subset \widetilde{\Omega }_t$$\end{document}B(0,1)⊂Ω~t. Through direct calculations, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \widetilde{\Omega }_t = B_{1+\frac{2}{\alpha }}\big (\tfrac{2}{\alpha },0\big )\quad \text{ for } \text{ every } t\in [0,T), $$\end{document}Ω~t=B1+2α(2α,0)foreveryt∈[0,T),and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\Omega }_t$$\end{document}Ω~t satisfies the desired properties. To verify (C3), i.e. that v(x, t) is a subsolution of (1.8) for appropriate values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0, we proceed as follows. By the properties of f and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*$$\end{document}f∗ stated in Proposition 6.1, it immediately follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Delta v={{\,\textrm{div}\,}}_x\nabla _\xi f(\nabla v)=\alpha $$\end{document}Δv=divx∇ξf(∇v)=α in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_1(0)\times [0,T)$$\end{document}B1(0)×[0,T). By a straightforward computation, we obtain for the time derivative of v that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t v(x,t)&=-x_1\sin t+x_2\cos t . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv(x,t)=-x1sint+x2cost.We can conclude that v is a subsolution of (1.8) in the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_1(0)\times [0,T)$$\end{document}B1(0)×[0,T) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 1$$\end{document}α≥1. Moreover, we estimate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nabla v|\le 2+\frac{\alpha }{2}$$\end{document}|∇v|≤2+α2 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_1(0)\times [0,T)$$\end{document}B1(0)×[0,T).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x_o,t)=g(x_o,t)$$\end{document}v(xo,t)=g(xo,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T);

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x_o,t)=g(x_o,t)$$\end{document}v(xo,t)=g(xo,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T);

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x,t)\le g(x,t)$$\end{document}v(x,t)≤g(x,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\big (B_1(0)\times [0,T)\big )$$\end{document}(x,t)∈∂P(B1(0)×[0,T));

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v(x,t)\le g(x,t)$$\end{document}v(x,t)≤g(x,t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\in \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\big (B_1(0)\times [0,T)\big )$$\end{document}(x,t)∈∂P(B1(0)×[0,T));

v is a sub-solution of 1.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t v-\Delta v =0 \qquad \text { in } B_1(0)\times [0,T). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv-Δv=0inB1(0)×[0,T).

v is a sub-solution of 1.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t v-\Delta v =0 \qquad \text { in } B_1(0)\times [0,T). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv-Δv=0inB1(0)×[0,T).

To be more precise, in the picture on the left there are the affine function involved in the Bounded Slope Condition for the point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_o=(-1,0)$$\end{document}xo=(-1,0) and the corresponding barrier; the continuous line is the boundary condition, while the dashed line is the trace of the barrier function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \widetilde{\Omega }_0$$\end{document}∂Ω~0.
