Similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x_o}^+$$\end{document}vxo+ is a super-minimizer. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{x_o}^\pm $$\end{document}vxo± are Lipschitz continuous with respect to the spatial variable with Lipschitz constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{Q}$$\end{document}Q~ depending on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n, \varepsilon , R, {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(\Omega ), f, \nabla f$$\end{document}n,ε,R,diam(Ω),f,∇f, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert D^2f\Vert _{L^\infty (\mathbb {R}^n\setminus B_1)}, Q, [g]_{0,1;\Omega _T}, \mathsf Q$$\end{document}‖D2f‖L∞(Rn\B1),Q,[g]0,1;ΩT,Q. Then, the maximum principle from Lemma 6.7 implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v_{x_o}^-\le u\le v_{x_o}^+\qquad \text{ in } \Omega _T, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}vxo-≤u≤vxo+inΩT,so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\widetilde{Q}|y|&\le v_{x_o}^+(x_o,t)- v_{x_o}^+(x_o+y,t) \\&\le g(x_o,t)- u(x_o+y,t)\\&\le v_{x_o}^-(x_o,t)- v_{x_o}^-(x_o+y,t) \le \widetilde{Q}|y|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Q~|y|≤vxo+(xo,t)-vxo+(xo+y,t)≤g(xo,t)-u(xo+y,t)≤vxo-(xo,t)-vxo-(xo+y,t)≤Q~|y|.Therefore, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |u(x_1,t)-u(x_2,t)| \le \widetilde{Q}|y|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|u(x1,t)-u(x2,t)|≤Q~|y|.Joining both cases and recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=x_2-x_1$$\end{document}y=x2-x1, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |u(x_1,t)-u(x_2,t)| \le \max \big \{ \widetilde{Q}, \Vert \nabla g_o\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega ,\mathbb {R}^n)}\big \} |x_1-x_2|, $$\end{document}|u(x1,t)-u(x2,t)|≤max{Q~,‖∇go‖L∞(Ω,Rn)}|x1-x2|,which implies the claimed gradient bound. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof of Theorem 1.3

In this section, we indicate how the gradient hypothesis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \nabla u\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega ,\mathbb {R}^n)}\le L$$\end{document}‖∇u‖L∞(Ω,Rn)≤L for a variational solution u of the gradient-constrained obstacle problem in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Lip}^x_{g}(\Omega _{T}, L)$$\end{document}Lipgx(ΩT,L) can be removed, thereby obtaining the existence result from Theorem 1.3.

Proof of Theorem 1.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L>C$$\end{document}L>C, where C denotes the constant from Proposition 6.8 depending only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n, \varepsilon , R, {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(\Omega ){, f,} \nabla f, \Vert D^2f\Vert _{L^\infty (\mathbb {R}^n\setminus B_1)}, Q, [g]_{0,1;\Omega _T}, \mathsf Q$$\end{document}n,ε,R,diam(Ω),f,∇f,‖D2f‖L∞(Rn\B1),Q,[g]0,1;ΩT,Q. Due to Proposition 5.1 there exists a unique variational solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in \textrm{Lip}^x_g(\Omega _T,L)$$\end{document}u∈Lipgx(ΩT,L) of the gradient constrained problem. The solution admits a weak time derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tu\in L^2(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}∂tu∈L2(ΩT) and satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(0)=g_o$$\end{document}u(0)=go in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\Omega )$$\end{document}L2(Ω)-sense. As pointed out at the beginning of § 6.3, u is also a parabolic minimizer in the sense of Definition 6.4. Proposition 6.8 ensures that the strict gradient bound\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \nabla u\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)} \le C < L $$\end{document}‖∇u‖L∞(ΩT,Rn)≤C<Lholds. Therefore, it remains to prove that the variational inequality (1.6) satisfied by u actually holds for any comparison map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \textrm{Lip}^x_{g}(\Omega _{T})$$\end{document}w∈Lipgx(ΩT) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t w\in L^2(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}∂tw∈L2(ΩT). To this aim we consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \textrm{Lip}^x_{g}(\Omega _{T})$$\end{document}w∈Lipgx(ΩT) and define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ v:= u+s(w-u)\qquad \text{ for } 0<s\ll 1. $$\end{document}v:=u+s(w-u)for0<s≪1.Observe that v is an admissible comparison function in (6.21), since v coincides with u on the lateral boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Omega \times (0,T)$$\end{document}∂Ω×(0,T) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \nabla v\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)}<L$$\end{document}‖∇v‖L∞(ΩT,Rn)<L for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>0$$\end{document}s>0 small enough. From (6.21) and the convexity of f we infer\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iint _{\Omega _T}f(\nabla u)\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t&\le \iint _{\Omega _T}\big [s\partial _{t}u(w-u) + f\big ( (1-s)\nabla u+s\nabla w\big )\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\\&\le \iint _{\Omega _T}\big [s\partial _{t}u(w-u) + (1-s) f(\nabla u)+sf(\nabla w)\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∬ΩTf(∇u)dxdt≤∬ΩT[s∂tu(w-u)+f((1-s)∇u+s∇w)]dxdt≤∬ΩT[s∂tu(w-u)+(1-s)f(∇u)+sf(∇w)]dxdt.We re-absorb the second term of the right-hand side into the left and divide the result by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>0$$\end{document}s>0, so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iint _{\Omega _T}f(\nabla u)\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t&\le \iint _{\Omega _T}\big [\partial _{t}u(w-u) + f(Dw)\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\\&= \iint _{\Omega _T}\big [\partial _{t}w(w-u)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial _t|w-u|^2 + f(\nabla w)\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\\&= \iint _{\Omega _T}\big [\partial _{t}w(w-u) + f(\nabla w)\big ]\,\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}t\\&\quad + \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert w(0)-g_{o}\Vert _{L^{2}(\Omega )}^{2} - \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert (w-u)(T)\Vert _{L^{2}(\Omega )}^{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∬ΩTf(∇u)dxdt≤∬ΩT[∂tu(w-u)+f(Dw)]dxdt=∬ΩT[∂tw(w-u)-12∂t|w-u|2+f(∇w)]dxdt=∬ΩT[∂tw(w-u)+f(∇w)]dxdt+12‖w(0)-go‖L2(Ω)2-12‖(w-u)(T)‖L2(Ω)2.This shows that the variational inequality (1.6) holds for every comparison function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \textrm{Lip}^x_{g}(\Omega _{T})$$\end{document}w∈Lipgx(ΩT) that satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tw\in L^2(\Omega _T)$$\end{document}∂tw∈L2(ΩT). Therefore, u is a variational solution of the unconstrained problem in the sense of Definition 1.1. As before, the uniqueness can be deduced as in [9, Lemma 3.3] and the proof of Theorem 1.3 is complete. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Regularity of Solutions and Proof of Theorem 1.4

Our aim in this section is to prove Theorem 1.4. By u we denote the unique variational solution from Theorem 1.3 satisfying\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \nabla u\Vert _{L^\infty (\Omega _T,\mathbb {R}^n)} \le C =: M. $$\end{document}‖∇u‖L∞(ΩT,Rn)≤C=:M.Since f is assumed to be of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla u$$\end{document}∇u is bounded, the associated Euler-Lagrange equation is well defined. Hence, u is a weak solution of the parabolic Cauchy-Dirichlet problem (1.3). From the Poincaré inequality for solutions to parabolic equations (cf. [8, Lemma 3.1]), we obtainfor any parabolic cylinder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\varrho (z_o):=B_\varrho (x_o)\times (t_o-\varrho ^2,t_o+\rho ^2)\subset \Omega _T$$\end{document}Qϱ(zo):=Bϱ(xo)×(to-ϱ2,to+ρ2)⊂ΩT with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_o=(x_o,t_o)\in \Omega _T$$\end{document}zo=(xo,to)∈ΩT. A similar Poincaré inequality holds for parabolic cylinders with center \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_o,t_o)\in \partial _{\mathcal {P}}\Omega _T$$\end{document}(xo,to)∈∂PΩT. This can be deduced as in [7], since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω is convex and therefore also a Lipschitz domain. The parabolic version of Campanato’s characterization of Hölder continuity (with respect to the parabolic metric) by Da Prato [16] implies the Lipschitz continuity of u with respect to the parabolic metric, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^{0;1,\frac{1}{2}}(\overline{\Omega }_T)$$\end{document}u∈C0;1,12(Ω¯T). This finishes the proof of Theorem 1.4.
